Yeah, before this whole foggy, low feeling hit me, I did have a stretch where I felt... I don't know, almost too good? Like I was just on top of the world, feeling really powerful and capable of anything. But it was weird because one minute I'd be riding that high, and the next I'd just snap. Yeah, I definitely was. I just couldn't sit still. It was like... if I stopped moving for even a second, all this grief and heavy stuff would just catch up to me, you know? So I just kept moving. some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about sleep, because usually I'm out ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let's see... it all started about two weeks ago. This whole shift really kicked in right after my uncle died. We were actually pretty close when I was a kid. He used to take me fishing out at this lake a couple hours from here. Lake... Oconee, I think? Yeah, Lake Oconee. We'd get up at the crack of dawn, pack these terrible bologna sandwiches on white bread with way too much mustard. He had this old aluminum boat that leaked just a little bit, so I always had to bail water out with a plastic Folgers coffee can while he handled the motor. The motor was this vintage Evinrude, smelled like gas and oil, but he swore by it. He said they didn't make them like that anymore. We caught a huge bass out there once, must have been five pounds. He had it mounted and put it in his den... 

But yeah, the not sleeping and all that energy, that started right after he passed, about two weeks ago.

I was actually just at this one client's house, Mr. Henderson, he's such a sweet older guy, always wants to talk about his time in the Navy. He served on this huge aircraft carrier, I can't remember the name of it now, but he showed me pictures. Anyway, I keep forgetting what I was about to do when I'm there, which really isn't like me at all. I usually have my routine down pat. But yeah, the Navy pictures were really something, black and white, you know? But this foggy feeling, it's just throwing me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, it's just like I was saying with that foggy feeling. I sit down to do my client notes and I just can't focus, so I end up making these little mistakes. Or I'll walk into a room and completely forget what I was about to do. Which really isn't like me at all, you know? I usually have a mind like a steel trap. \n\nMy buddy Dave always says I should've been an accountant because I'm usually so good with details. Dave actually just bought this old Chevy truck he's trying to fix up—completely rusted out, but he thinks he can get it running by summer. I told him he's out of his mind, parts for those things cost an absolute fortune now, especially the transmission. He had to drive three states over just to find a bumper. But anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't seem to keep my thoughts straight enough to get my work done right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about sleep, because usually I'm out like a light by 10 PM. But lately... yeah, for several nights there I was only sleeping maybe three, four hours tops. And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, ready to go. I'd still make my morning coffee, but honestly, it was more just out of habit than actually needing it to wake up. \n\nI use this French press my sister got me for my birthday last year. She's really into all that artisanal coffee stuff, buys these beans from some little farm in Colombia. She actually went down there on vacation, said the mountains were unbelievable. Took a whole album of photos on her phone, mostly of these tiny little frogs she kept finding near her hotel. They were bright blue, if you can believe it. She said they're poisonous, but she got right up close anyway. Typical Sarah, always jumping into things without thinking. \n\nBut yeah, I'd just sit there with my fancy coffee at 4 AM, not even tired, just... buzzing, I guess. It was strange.\n\nDoctor: During those days when you were sleeping only a few hours, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah, before this whole foggy, low feeling hit me, I did have a stretch where I felt... I don't know, almost too good? Like I was just on top of the world, feeling really powerful and capable of anything. But it was weird because one minute I'd be riding that high, and the next I'd just snap. I'd get so sharp with my wife over the dumbest little things, or I'd bite my coworkers' heads off if they didn't do something exactly right. \n\nMy wife, she's usually so patient with me. We actually just celebrated our 15th anniversary. We went to this little Italian place downtown, the one with the red checkered tablecloths? They make this incredible lasagna, supposedly an old family recipe from Naples or somewhere. The owner came out and talked to us for like twenty minutes about his grandmother who started the place. He was wearing this wild tie, bright yellow with little green turtles all over it. I remember thinking, who wears a tie like that to run a restaurant? But the food was great. \n\nAnyway, yeah, I was feeling fantastic, just really on fire, but then I'd just get so irritable and snap at people. It was a weird mix.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely was. I just couldn't sit still. It was like... if I stopped moving for even a second, all this grief and heavy stuff would just catch up to me, you know? So I just kept moving. I was making lists, running errands, calling people I haven't talked to in years, doing chores around the house. \n\nI actually decided to finally clean out the gutters, which I've been putting off forever. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he saw me up on the ladder and came over to give me advice. He's always doing that, thinks he's the neighborhood handyman just because he used to work in construction back in the eighties. He was telling me about this special type of sealant he uses on his roof, went on and on about the chemical composition of it. Honestly, I think he just gets lonely since his kids moved out. His daughter just had a baby, actually, a little girl. They named her something unique... Juniper, I think? Or maybe Willow. One of those nature names that are so popular right now. \n\nBut yeah, I was just constantly finding things to do. If I wasn't doing something, I felt like I was going to explode.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely made things... messy. At work, I'm a personal care aide, so there's a lot of documentation for my clients. Lately, I've had to ask my coworkers to double-check my notes because I just keep missing things. Which reminds me, one of my clients, Mrs. Gable, she has this old grandfather clock in her hallway. Beautiful piece, dark mahogany, must be from the 1920s or something. It chimes every quarter hour, and she told me her late husband bought it at an antique auction in London. They used to travel everywhere before he passed. She showed me pictures of them in Egypt, riding camels right in front of the pyramids. The sky in the photos was this incredible, piercing blue... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at work I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. And at home, too. My wife has actually had to take over paying some of the bills and handling our schedule. She got this new planner, it's got these little stickers for different appointments. She's always been so organized, unlike me.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that energized, sleeping-only-3-to-4-hours period last?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it all started about two weeks ago. This whole shift really kicked in right after my uncle died. We were actually pretty close when I was a kid. He used to take me fishing out at this lake a couple hours from here. Lake... Oconee, I think? Yeah, Lake Oconee. We'd get up at the crack of dawn, pack these terrible bologna sandwiches on white bread with way too much mustard. He had this old aluminum boat that leaked just a little bit, so I always had to bail water out with a plastic Folgers coffee can while he handled the motor. The motor was this vintage Evinrude, smelled like gas and oil, but he swore by it. He said they didn't make them like that anymore. We caught a huge bass out there once, must have been five pounds. He had it mounted and put it in his den... \n\nBut yeah, the not sleeping and all that energy, that started right after he passed, about two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized stretch last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, this whole shift really started after my uncle died, which was about two weeks ago. And it's just been going ever since. Two weeks of just... zooming. It's funny, when my uncle died, they had this beautiful service for him. The floral arrangements were just incredible. There were these massive wreaths of white lilies and blue hydrangeas. My aunt, she insisted on hydrangeas because they were his mother's favorite flower. She grew them along the side of her house back in Ohio. The soil there must have been really acidic, because the flowers were always this deep, vibrant blue. I read once that you can change the color of hydrangeas by putting coffee grounds or pennies in the soil. I actually tried that in my own garden last spring, but the neighborhood stray cats kept digging it up. There's this one orange tabby that just won't leave my flowerbeds alone. Well, yeah, I did. I honestly believed I could just quit waiting around and start my own Spanish-speaking senior-care agency right away. I was making lists, running errands, calling people I haven't talked to in years, doing chores around the house. \n\nI actually decided to finally clean out the gutters, which I've been putting off forever. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he saw me up on the ladder and came over to give me advice. He's always doing that, thinks he's the neighborhood handyman just because he used to work in construction back in the eighties. He was telling me about this special type of sealant he uses on his roof, went on and on about the chemical composition of it. Honestly, I think he just gets lonely since his kids moved out. His daughter just had a baby, actually, a little girl. They named her something unique... Juniper, I think? Or maybe Willow. One of those nature names that are so popular right now. \n\nBut yeah, I was just constantly finding things to do. If I wasn't doing something, I felt like I was going to explode.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely made things... messy. At work, I'm a personal care aide, so there's a lot of documentation for my clients. Lately, I've had to ask my coworkers to double-check my notes because I just keep missing things. Which reminds me, one of my clients, Mrs. Gable, she has this old grandfather clock in her hallway. Beautiful piece, dark mahogany, must be from the 1920s or something. It chimes every quarter hour, and she told me her late husband bought it at an antique auction in London. They used to travel everywhere before he passed. She showed me pictures of them in Egypt, riding camels right in front of the pyramids. The sky in the photos was this incredible, piercing blue... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at work I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. And at home, too. My wife has actually had to take over paying some of the bills and handling our schedule. She got this new planner, it's got these little stickers for different appointments. She's always been so organized, unlike me.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that energized, sleeping-only-3-to-4-hours period last?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it all started about two weeks ago. This whole shift really kicked in right after my uncle died. We were actually pretty close when I was a kid. He used to take me fishing out at this lake a couple hours from here. Lake... Oconee, I think? Yeah, Lake Oconee. We'd get up at the crack of dawn, pack these terrible bologna sandwiches on white bread with way too much mustard. He had this old aluminum boat that leaked just a little bit, so I always had to bail water out with a plastic Folgers coffee can while he handled the motor. The motor was this vintage Evinrude, smelled like gas and oil, but he swore by it. He said they didn't make them like that anymore. We caught a huge bass out there once, must have been five pounds. He had it mounted and put it in his den... \n\nBut yeah, the not sleeping and all that energy, that started right after he passed, about two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized stretch last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's funny, when my uncle died, they had this beautiful service for him. The floral arrangements were just incredible. There were these massive wreaths of white lilies and blue hydrangeas. My aunt, she insisted on hydrangeas because they were his mother's favorite flower. She grew them along the side of her house back in Ohio. The soil there must have been really acidic, because the flowers were always this deep, vibrant blue. I read once that you can change the color of hydrangeas by putting coffee grounds or pennies in the soil. I actually tried that in my own garden last spring, but the neighborhood stray cats kept digging it up. There's this one orange tabby that just won't leave my flowerbeds alone. He's got a nick in his left ear, looks like he's been in a few scraps... but yeah, it's been about two weeks straight of this.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel more powerful or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I did. It was always so hot in there, the windows would steam up completely. She had this old cast-iron pot she used for the masa, must have weighed twenty pounds. He was the star goalie for our high school team. They actually went to the state championships that year, played against a team from up north that had these blindingly bright neon green jerseys. You could see them from a mile away... but yeah, I just felt like I had all the answers and could start that agency without a hitch.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. You could see them from a mile away... but yeah, I just felt like I had all the answers and could start that agency without a hitch.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that energized stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / personal_care_aide / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess maybe a little? I don't know, it's hard to tell when you're just going and going. But you know who did talk a lot? My high school speech teacher, Mr. Gable. That guy could talk for hours about absolutely nothing. He had this weird habit of jingling the change in his pocket while he lectured, and it drove everyone crazy. It was always pennies and quarters, just clinking away. I tried saving quarters once in this big plastic Coke bottle bank I won at the county fair. The fair was great back then, they had this ride called the Zipper that just flipped you upside down over and over. I threw up a funnel cake on that ride when I was twelve. Powdered sugar everywhere. My mom was so mad she made me ride home in the bed of my dad's pickup truck. It was an old Chevy C10, light blue, with a cracked windshield on the passenger side from a stray golf ball... but yeah, I'm not really sure if I was talking more than usual or not.

every phone buzz, hallway noise, or random idea in my head, and then I’d lose my place in the paperwork.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I was pushing hard on all these plans at once—memorial details, car repairs, CNA classes, business ideas—and calling relatives and coworkers more than I normally would.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: At work I’ve had to ask coworkers to double-check my client documentation, and at home my wife has taken over some bills and scheduling because I’m not as reliable with details right now.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: This whole shift really started after my uncle died, about two weeks ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The low, guilty, foggy feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / personal_care_aide / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S031\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Did that energized stretch last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, this whole shift really started after my uncle died, which was about two weeks ago. And it's just been going ever since. Two weeks of just... zooming. It's funny, when my uncle died, they had this beautiful service for him. The floral arrangements were just incredible. There were these massive wreaths of white lilies and blue hydrangeas. My aunt, she insisted on hydrangeas because they were his mother's favorite flower. She grew them along the side of her house back in Ohio. The soil there must have been really acidic, because the flowers were always this deep, vibrant blue. I read once that you can change the color of hydrangeas by putting coffee grounds or pennies in the soil. I actually tried that in my own garden last spring, but the neighborhood stray cats kept digging it up. There's this one orange tabby that just won't leave my flowerbeds alone. He's got a nick in his left ear, looks like he's been in a few scraps... but yeah, it's been about two weeks straight of this.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel more powerful or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I did. I honestly believed I could just quit waiting around and start my own Spanish-speaking senior-care agency right away. Like, I had it all figured out in my head and I just couldn't fail. It made so much sense. I mean, my grandmother spoke Spanish, and she used to make these amazing tamales every Christmas. The whole family would get together in her tiny kitchen. It was always so hot in there, the windows would steam up completely. She had this old cast-iron pot she used for the masa, must have weighed twenty pounds. My cousin Hector dropped it on his toe once when we were kids, broke it right in two places. He had to wear this clunky black boot for six weeks, which really messed up his soccer season. He was the star goalie for our high school team. They actually went to the state championships that year, played against a team from up north that had these blindingly bright neon green jerseys. You could see them from a mile away... but yeah, I just felt like I had all the answers and could start that agency without a hitch.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess maybe a little? I don't know, it's hard to tell when you're just going and going. But you know who did talk a lot? My high school speech teacher, Mr. Gable. That guy could talk for hours about absolutely nothing. He had this weird habit of jingling the change in his pocket while he lectured, and it drove everyone crazy. It was always pennies and quarters, just clinking away. I tried saving quarters once in this big plastic Coke bottle bank I won at the county fair. The fair was great back then, they had this ride called the Zipper that just flipped you upside down over and over. I threw up a funnel cake on that ride when I was twelve. Powdered sugar everywhere. My mom was so mad she made me ride home in the bed of my dad's pickup truck. It was an old Chevy C10, light blue, with a cracked windshield on the passenger side from a stray golf ball... but yeah, I'm not really sure if I was talking more than usual or not.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that energized period, did you have any hallucinations or beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. No, I didn't see anything that wasn't there, or think things that people said were crazy. I mean, my sister always tells me my ideas are crazy, but she's just like that. She's been super critical ever since we were kids. Like this one time, we were building a treehouse in the big oak out back, and she kept saying my knots weren't tied right. I learned how to tie knots in the Boy Scouts, actually. We had this scoutmaster, Mr. Henderson, who used to take us camping up near the lake. The mosquitoes there were just huge, like the size of small birds. I got bit so many times on my ankles that summer I had to wear these thick wool socks even though it was ninety degrees out. Wool socks are the worst when it's hot, they make your feet sweat so much and then they smell like wet dog. We had a dog, a golden retriever named Buster, who loved swimming in that lake... but no, I didn't have anything like what you're asking about.

He's always doing that, thinks he's the neighborhood handyman just because he used to work in construction back in the eighties. He was telling me about this special type of sealant he uses on his roof, went on and on about the chemical composition of it. Honestly, I think he just gets lonely since his kids moved out. His daughter just had a baby, actually, a little girl. They named her something unique... Juniper, I think? Or maybe Willow. One of those nature names that are so popular right now. \n\nBut yeah, I was just constantly finding things to do. If I wasn't doing something, I felt like I was going to explode.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely made things... messy. At work, I'm a personal care aide, so there's a lot of documentation for my clients. Lately, I've had to ask my coworkers to double-check my notes because I just keep missing things. Which reminds me, one of my clients, Mrs. Gable, she has this old grandfather clock in her hallway. Beautiful piece, dark mahogany, must be from the 1920s or something. It chimes every quarter hour, and she told me her late husband bought it at an antique auction in London. They used to travel everywhere before he passed. She showed me pictures of them in Egypt, riding camels right in front of the pyramids. The sky in the photos was this incredible, piercing blue... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at work I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. And at home, too. My wife has actually had to take over paying some of the bills and handling our schedule. She got this new planner, it's got these little stickers for different appointments. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he saw me up on the ladder and came over to give me advice. He's always doing that, thinks he's the neighborhood handyman just because he used to work in construction back in the eighties. He was telling me about this special type of sealant he uses on his roof, went on and on about the chemical composition of it. Honestly, I think he just gets lonely since his kids moved out. His daughter just had a baby, actually, a little girl. They named her something unique... Juniper, I think? Or maybe Willow. One of those nature names that are so popular right now. \n\nBut yeah, I was just constantly finding things to do. If I wasn't doing something, I felt like I was going to explode.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely made things... messy. At work, I'm a personal care aide, so there's a lot of documentation for my clients. Lately, I've had to ask my coworkers to double-check my notes because I just keep missing things. Which reminds me, one of my clients, Mrs. Gable, she has this old grandfather clock in her hallway. Beautiful piece, dark mahogany, must be from the 1920s or something. It chimes every quarter hour, and she told me her late husband bought it at an antique auction in London. They used to travel everywhere before he passed. She showed me pictures of them in Egypt, riding camels right in front of the pyramids. The sky in the photos was this incredible, piercing blue... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at work I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. And at home, too. My wife has actually had to take over paying some of the bills and handling our schedule. She got this new planner, it's got these little stickers for different appointments. She's always been so organized, unlike me.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that energized, sleeping-only-3-to-4-hours period last?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it all started about two weeks ago. This whole shift really kicked in right after my uncle died. We were actually pretty close when I was a kid. He used to take me fishing out at this lake a couple hours from here. Lake... Oconee, I think? Yeah, Lake Oconee. We'd get up at the crack of dawn, pack these terrible bologna sandwiches on white bread with way too much mustard. He had this old aluminum boat that leaked just a little bit, so I always had to bail water out with a plastic Folgers coffee can while he handled the motor. The motor was this vintage Evinrude, smelled like gas and oil, but he swore by it. He said they didn't make them like that anymore. We caught a huge bass out there once, must have been five pounds. He had it mounted and put it in his den... \n\nBut yeah, the not sleeping and all that energy, that started right after he passed, about two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized stretch last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, this whole shift really started after my uncle died, which was about two weeks ago. And it's just been going ever since. Two weeks of just... zooming. It's funny, when my uncle died, they had this beautiful service for him. The floral arrangements were just incredible. There were these massive wreaths of white lilies and blue hydrangeas. My aunt, she insisted on hydrangeas because they were his mother's favorite flower. She grew them along the side of her house back in Ohio. The soil there must have been really acidic, because the flowers were always this deep, vibrant blue. I read once that you can change the color of hydrangeas by putting coffee grounds or pennies in the soil. I actually tried that in my own garden last spring, but the neighborhood stray cats kept digging it up. There's this one orange tabby that just won't leave my flowerbeds alone. He's got a nick in his left ear, looks like he's been in a few scraps... but yeah, it's been about two weeks straight of this.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel more powerful or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I did. I honestly believed I could just quit waiting around and start my own Spanish-speaking senior-care agency right away. Like, I had it all figured out in my head and I just couldn't fail. It made so much sense. I mean, my grandmother spoke Spanish, and she used to make these amazing tamales every Christmas. The whole family would get together in her tiny kitchen. It was always so hot in there, the windows would steam up completely. She had this old cast-iron pot she used for the masa, must have weighed twenty pounds. My cousin Hector dropped it on his toe once when we were kids, broke it right in two places. He had to wear this clunky black boot for six weeks, which really messed up his soccer season. He was the star goalie for our high school team. They actually went to the state championships that year, played against a team from up north that had these blindingly bright neon green jerseys. You could see them from a mile away... but yeah, I just felt like I had all the answers and could start that agency without a hitch.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess maybe a little? I don't know, it's hard to tell when you're just going and going. But you know who did talk a lot? My high school speech teacher, Mr. Gable. That guy could talk for hours about absolutely nothing. He had this weird habit of jingling the change in his pocket while he lectured, and it drove everyone crazy. It was always pennies and quarters, just clinking away. I tried saving quarters once in this big plastic Coke bottle bank I won at the county fair. The fair was great back then, they had this ride called the Zipper that just flipped you upside down over and over. I threw up a funnel cake on that ride when I was twelve. Powdered sugar everywhere. My mom was so mad she made me ride home in the bed of my dad's pickup truck. It was an old Chevy C10, light blue, with a cracked windshield on the passenger side from a stray golf ball... but yeah, I'm not really sure if I was talking more than usual or not.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you have any hallucinations or beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't see anything that wasn't there, or think things that people said were crazy. I got bit so many times on my ankles that summer I had to wear these thick wool socks even though it was ninety degrees out. Wool socks are the worst when it's hot, they make your feet sweat so much and then they smell like wet dog. No, I don't think so. I mean, not that I can remember before he passed. His funeral was really hard, actually. Which reminds me, one of my clients, Mrs. Gable, she has this old grandfather clock in her hallway. Beautiful piece, dark mahogany, must be from the 1920s or something. It chimes every quarter hour, and she told me her late husband bought it at an antique auction in London. They used to travel everywhere before he passed. She showed me pictures of them in Egypt, riding camels right in front of the pyramids. The sky in the photos was this incredible, piercing blue... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at work I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. And at home, too. My wife has actually had to take over paying some of the bills and handling our schedule. She got this new planner, it's got these little stickers for different appointments. She's always been so organized, unlike me.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that energized, sleeping-only-3-to-4-hours period last?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it all started about two weeks ago. This whole shift really kicked in right after my uncle died. We were actually pretty close when I was a kid. He used to take me fishing out at this lake a couple hours from here. Lake... Oconee, I think? Yeah, Lake Oconee. We'd get up at the crack of dawn, pack these terrible bologna sandwiches on white bread with way too much mustard. He had this old aluminum boat that leaked just a little bit, so I always had to bail water out with a plastic Folgers coffee can while he handled the motor. The motor was this vintage Evinrude, smelled like gas and oil, but he swore by it. He said they didn't make them like that anymore. We caught a huge bass out there once, must have been five pounds. He had it mounted and put it in his den... \n\nBut yeah, the not sleeping and all that energy, that started right after he passed, about two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized stretch last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, this whole shift really started after my uncle died, which was about two weeks ago. And it's just been going ever since. Two weeks of just... zooming. It's funny, when my uncle died, they had this beautiful service for him. The floral arrangements were just incredible. There were these massive wreaths of white lilies and blue hydrangeas. My aunt, she insisted on hydrangeas because they were his mother's favorite flower. She grew them along the side of her house back in Ohio. The soil there must have been really acidic, because the flowers were always this deep, vibrant blue. I read once that you can change the color of hydrangeas by putting coffee grounds or pennies in the soil. I actually tried that in my own garden last spring, but the neighborhood stray cats kept digging it up. There's this one orange tabby that just won't leave my flowerbeds alone. He's got a nick in his left ear, looks like he's been in a few scraps... but yeah, it's been about two weeks straight of this.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel more powerful or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I did. It was always so hot in there, the windows would steam up completely. She had this old cast-iron pot she used for the masa, must have weighed twenty pounds. My cousin Hector dropped it on his toe once when we were kids, broke it right in two places. He had to wear this clunky black boot for six weeks, which really messed up his soccer season. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't know, honestly. I mean, everybody handles grief differently, right? When my aunt passed, my cousin cried for like three weeks straight. She couldn't even go to work. She worked at this bakery downtown, the one that makes those amazing blueberry scones. I used to go there every Tuesday morning before my shift. I'm a personal care aide, so I have to be up pretty early to help my clients get out of bed and dressed. The dog is practically deaf, but he knows the exact sound of that toaster. He loves toast with a little bit of butter on it. Real butter, not the margarine stuff. Margarine is basically just oil and water anyway, I saw a documentary about how they make it in these massive vats... but yeah, I couldn't really tell you if the way I reacted was normal or not.

My aunt, she insisted on hydrangeas because they were his mother's favorite flower. She grew them along the side of her house back in Ohio. The soil there must have been really acidic, because the flowers were always this deep, vibrant blue. I read once that you can change the color of hydrangeas by putting coffee grounds or pennies in the soil. I actually tried that in my own garden last spring, but the neighborhood stray cats kept digging it up. There's this one orange tabby that just won't leave my flowerbeds alone. He's got a nick in his left ear, looks like he's been in a few scraps... but yeah, it's been about two weeks straight of this.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel more powerful or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I did. My cousin Hector dropped it on his toe once when we were kids, broke it right in two places. He had to wear this clunky black boot for six weeks, which really messed up his soccer season. I don't really remember my wife or the guys at work saying anything about me acting different. My wife was pretty distracted anyway because we were dealing with this leak in the downstairs bathroom. But lately... yeah, for several nights there I was only sleeping maybe three, four hours tops. And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, ready to go. I'd still make my morning coffee, but honestly, it was more just out of habit than actually needing it to wake up. \n\nI use this French press my sister got me for my birthday last year. She's really into all that artisanal coffee stuff, buys these beans from some little farm in Colombia. She actually went down there on vacation, said the mountains were unbelievable. Took a whole album of photos on her phone, mostly of these tiny little frogs she kept finding near her hotel. They were bright blue, if you can believe it. She said they're poisonous, but she got right up close anyway. Typical Sarah, always jumping into things without thinking. \n\nBut yeah, I'd just sit there with my fancy coffee at 4 AM, not even tired, just... buzzing, I guess. It was strange.\n\nDoctor: During those days when you were sleeping only a few hours, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah, before this whole foggy, low feeling hit me, I did have a stretch where I felt... But it was weird because one minute I'd be riding that high, and the next I'd just snap. They make this incredible lasagna, supposedly an old family recipe from Naples or somewhere. The owner came out and talked to us for like twenty minutes about his grandmother who started the place. He was wearing this wild tie, bright yellow with little green turtles all over it. I remember thinking, who wears a tie like that to run a restaurant? But the food was great. \n\nAnyway, yeah, I was feeling fantastic, just really on fire, but then I'd just get so irritable and snap at people. It was a weird mix.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely was. I just couldn't sit still. It was like... if I stopped moving for even a second, all this grief and heavy stuff would just catch up to me, you know? So I just kept moving. I was making lists, running errands, calling people I haven't talked to in years, doing chores around the house. \n\nI actually decided to finally clean out the gutters, which I've been putting off forever. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he saw me up on the ladder and came over to give me advice. He's always doing that, thinks he's the neighborhood handyman just because he used to work in construction back in the eighties. He was telling me about this special type of sealant he uses on his roof, went on and on about the chemical composition of it. Honestly, I think he just gets lonely since his kids moved out. His daughter just had a baby, actually, a little girl. They named her something unique... Juniper, I think? Or maybe Willow. One of those nature names that are so popular right now. \n\nBut yeah, I was just constantly finding things to do. If I wasn't doing something, I felt like I was going to explode.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely made things... messy. At work, I'm a personal care aide, so there's a lot of documentation for my clients. Lately, I've had to ask my coworkers to double-check my notes because I just keep missing things. Which reminds me, one of my clients, Mrs. Gable, she has this old grandfather clock in her hallway. Beautiful piece, dark mahogany, must be from the 1920s or something. It chimes every quarter hour, and she told me her late husband bought it at an antique auction in London. They used to travel everywhere before he passed. She showed me pictures of them in Egypt, riding camels right in front of the pyramids. The sky in the photos was this incredible, piercing blue... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at work I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. And at home, too. My wife has actually had to take over paying some of the bills and handling our schedule. She got this new planner, it's got these little stickers for different appointments. She's always been so organized, unlike me.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that energized, sleeping-only-3-to-4-hours period last?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it all started about two weeks ago. This whole shift really kicked in right after my uncle died. We were actually pretty close when I was a kid. He used to take me fishing out at this lake a couple hours from here. Lake... Oconee, I think? Yeah, Lake Oconee. We'd get up at the crack of dawn, pack these terrible bologna sandwiches on white bread with way too much mustard. He had this old aluminum boat that leaked just a little bit, so I always had to bail water out with a plastic Folgers coffee can while he handled the motor. The motor was this vintage Evinrude, smelled like gas and oil, but he swore by it. He said they didn't make them like that anymore. We caught a huge bass out there once, must have been five pounds. He had it mounted and put it in his den... \n\nBut yeah, the not sleeping and all that energy, that started right after he passed, about two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized stretch last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, this whole shift really started after my uncle died, which was about two weeks ago. And it's just been going ever since. Two weeks of just... zooming. It's funny, when my uncle died, they had this beautiful service for him. The floral arrangements were just incredible. There were these massive wreaths of white lilies and blue hydrangeas. My aunt, she insisted on hydrangeas because they were his mother's favorite flower. She grew them along the side of her house back in Ohio. The soil there must have been really acidic, because the flowers were always this deep, vibrant blue. I read once that you can change the color of hydrangeas by putting coffee grounds or pennies in the soil. I actually tried that in my own garden last spring, but the neighborhood stray cats kept digging it up. There's this one orange tabby that just won't leave my flowerbeds alone. He's got a nick in his left ear, looks like he's been in a few scraps... but yeah, it's been about two weeks straight of this.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel more powerful or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I did. I honestly believed I could just quit waiting around and start my own Spanish-speaking senior-care agency right away. Like, I had it all figured out in my head and I just couldn't fail. It made so much sense. I mean, my grandmother spoke Spanish, and she used to make these amazing tamales every Christmas. The whole family would get together in her tiny kitchen. It was always so hot in there, the windows would steam up completely. She had this old cast-iron pot she used for the masa, must have weighed twenty pounds. My cousin Hector dropped it on his toe once when we were kids, broke it right in two places. He had to wear this clunky black boot for six weeks, which really messed up his soccer season. He was the star goalie for our high school team. They actually went to the state championships that year, played against a team from up north that had these blindingly bright neon green jerseys. You could see them from a mile away... but yeah, I just felt like I had all the answers and could start that agency without a hitch.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess maybe a little? I don't know, it's hard to tell when you're just going and going. But you know who did talk a lot? My high school speech teacher, Mr. Gable. That guy could talk for hours about absolutely nothing. He had this weird habit of jingling the change in his pocket while he lectured, and it drove everyone crazy. It was always pennies and quarters, just clinking away. I tried saving quarters once in this big plastic Coke bottle bank I won at the county fair. The fair was great back then, they had this ride called the Zipper that just flipped you upside down over and over. I threw up a funnel cake on that ride when I was twelve. Powdered sugar everywhere. My mom was so mad she made me ride home in the bed of my dad's pickup truck. It was an old Chevy C10, light blue, with a cracked windshield on the passenger side from a stray golf ball... but yeah, I'm not really sure if I was talking more than usual or not.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you have any hallucinations or beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't see anything that wasn't there, or think things that people said were crazy. I mean, my sister always tells me my ideas are crazy, but she's just like that. She's been super critical ever since we were kids. Like this one time, we were building a treehouse in the big oak out back, and she kept saying my knots weren't tied right. I learned how to tie knots in the Boy Scouts, actually. We had this scoutmaster, Mr. Henderson, who used to take us camping up near the lake. The mosquitoes there were just huge, like the size of small birds. I got bit so many times on my ankles that summer I had to wear these thick wool socks even though it was ninety degrees out. Wool socks are the worst when it's hot, they make your feet sweat so much and then they smell like wet dog. We drove all the way down there in a Honda Civic, four of us crammed in. The AC broke somewhere in Georgia, and we had to roll the windows down, which just blew all this hot, humid air in our faces. My buddy Dave was in the backseat, and he kept complaining about his knees being cramped. He's like six-foot-three, played basketball for a community college in Ohio. They actually made it to some regional tournament, but he sprained his ankle right before the first game. He was so bummed out, he just sat on the bench eating those little bags of pretzels they sell at the concession stand... but yeah, before my uncle, I don't remember ever feeling that revved up or skipping sleep like that.\n\nDoctor: Did this burst of energy and confidence feel clearly unlike the kind of reaction you would normally have after a loss?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I'm a personal care aide, so I have to be up pretty early to help my clients get out of bed and dressed. No, I don't think I did anything really risky. I mean, I'm usually a pretty careful guy. Like, I always check the tire pressure on my car before a long trip. You know, you can get better gas mileage if your tires are inflated right. I read this article once that said most people are driving around on under-inflated tires and wasting all this money on gas. Gas prices are just crazy right now, anyway. I remember when I first started driving, I could fill up my tank for like fifteen bucks. It was a '89 Ford Escort, manual transmission. I sit down to do my client notes and I just can't focus, so I end up making these little mistakes. Or I'll walk into a room and completely forget what I was about to do. Which really isn't like me at all, you know? I usually have a mind like a steel trap. \n\nMy buddy Dave always says I should've been an accountant because I'm usually so good with details. Dave actually just bought this old Chevy truck he's trying to fix up—completely rusted out, but he thinks he can get it running by summer. I told him he's out of his mind, parts for those things cost an absolute fortune now, especially the transmission. He had to drive three states over just to find a bumper. But anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, right, concentrating. Yeah, I just can't seem to keep my thoughts straight enough to get my work done right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about sleep, because usually I'm out like a light by 10 PM. But lately... yeah, for several nights there I was only sleeping maybe three, four hours tops. And the weird thing is, I'd wake up feeling totally charged. Like, ready to go. I'd still make my morning coffee, but honestly, it was more just out of habit than actually needing it to wake up. \n\nI use this French press my sister got me for my birthday last year. She's really into all that artisanal coffee stuff, buys these beans from some little farm in Colombia. She actually went down there on vacation, said the mountains were unbelievable. Took a whole album of photos on her phone, mostly of these tiny little frogs she kept finding near her hotel. They were bright blue, if you can believe it. She said they're poisonous, but she got right up close anyway. Typical Sarah, always jumping into things without thinking. \n\nBut yeah, I'd just sit there with my fancy coffee at 4 AM, not even tired, just... buzzing, I guess. It was strange.\n\nDoctor: During those days when you were sleeping only a few hours, did you feel unusually happy or euphoric?\n\nPatient: Yeah, before this whole foggy, low feeling hit me, I did have a stretch where I felt... I don't know, almost too good? Like I was just on top of the world, feeling really powerful and capable of anything. But it was weird because one minute I'd be riding that high, and the next I'd just snap. I'd get so sharp with my wife over the dumbest little things, or I'd bite my coworkers' heads off if they didn't do something exactly right. \n\nMy wife, she's usually so patient with me. We actually just celebrated our 15th anniversary. We went to this little Italian place downtown, the one with the red checkered tablecloths? They make this incredible lasagna, supposedly an old family recipe from Naples or somewhere. The owner came out and talked to us for like twenty minutes about his grandmother who started the place. He was wearing this wild tie, bright yellow with little green turtles all over it. I remember thinking, who wears a tie like that to run a restaurant? But the food was great. \n\nAnyway, yeah, I was feeling fantastic, just really on fire, but then I'd just get so irritable and snap at people. It was a weird mix.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you more active than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely was. I just couldn't sit still. It was like... if I stopped moving for even a second, all this grief and heavy stuff would just catch up to me, you know? So I just kept moving. I was making lists, running errands, calling people I haven't talked to in years, doing chores around the house. \n\nI actually decided to finally clean out the gutters, which I've been putting off forever. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he saw me up on the ladder and came over to give me advice. He's always doing that, thinks he's the neighborhood handyman just because he used to work in construction back in the eighties. He was telling me about this special type of sealant he uses on his roof, went on and on about the chemical composition of it. Honestly, I think he just gets lonely since his kids moved out. His daughter just had a baby, actually, a little girl. They named her something unique... Juniper, I think? Or maybe Willow. One of those nature names that are so popular right now. \n\nBut yeah, I was just constantly finding things to do. If I wasn't doing something, I felt like I was going to explode.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did it cause serious problems at work or home?\n\nPatient: Well, it definitely made things... messy. At work, I'm a personal care aide, so there's a lot of documentation for my clients. Lately, I've had to ask my coworkers to double-check my notes because I just keep missing things. Which reminds me, one of my clients, Mrs. Gable, she has this old grandfather clock in her hallway. Beautiful piece, dark mahogany, must be from the 1920s or something. It chimes every quarter hour, and she told me her late husband bought it at an antique auction in London. They used to travel everywhere before he passed. She showed me pictures of them in Egypt, riding camels right in front of the pyramids. The sky in the photos was this incredible, piercing blue... \n\nBut anyway, yeah, at work I'm just not as reliable with the details right now. And at home, too. My wife has actually had to take over paying some of the bills and handling our schedule. She got this new planner, it's got these little stickers for different appointments. She's always been so organized, unlike me.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that energized, sleeping-only-3-to-4-hours period last?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it all started about two weeks ago. This whole shift really kicked in right after my uncle died. We were actually pretty close when I was a kid. He used to take me fishing out at this lake a couple hours from here. Lake... Oconee, I think? Yeah, Lake Oconee. We'd get up at the crack of dawn, pack these terrible bologna sandwiches on white bread with way too much mustard. He had this old aluminum boat that leaked just a little bit, so I always had to bail water out with a plastic Folgers coffee can while he handled the motor. The motor was this vintage Evinrude, smelled like gas and oil, but he swore by it. He said they didn't make them like that anymore. We caught a huge bass out there once, must have been five pounds. He had it mounted and put it in his den... \n\nBut yeah, the not sleeping and all that energy, that started right after he passed, about two weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Did that energized stretch last at least 4 days in a row?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Like I said, this whole shift really started after my uncle died, which was about two weeks ago. And it's just been going ever since. Two weeks of just... zooming. It's funny, when my uncle died, they had this beautiful service for him. The floral arrangements were just incredible. There were these massive wreaths of white lilies and blue hydrangeas. My aunt, she insisted on hydrangeas because they were his mother's favorite flower. She grew them along the side of her house back in Ohio. The soil there must have been really acidic, because the flowers were always this deep, vibrant blue. I read once that you can change the color of hydrangeas by putting coffee grounds or pennies in the soil. I actually tried that in my own garden last spring, but the neighborhood stray cats kept digging it up. There's this one orange tabby that just won't leave my flowerbeds alone. He's got a nick in his left ear, looks like he's been in a few scraps... but yeah, it's been about two weeks straight of this.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, did you feel more powerful or important than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I did. I honestly believed I could just quit waiting around and start my own Spanish-speaking senior-care agency right away. Like, I had it all figured out in my head and I just couldn't fail. It made so much sense. I mean, my grandmother spoke Spanish, and she used to make these amazing tamales every Christmas. The whole family would get together in her tiny kitchen. It was always so hot in there, the windows would steam up completely. She had this old cast-iron pot she used for the masa, must have weighed twenty pounds. My cousin Hector dropped it on his toe once when we were kids, broke it right in two places. He had to wear this clunky black boot for six weeks, which really messed up his soccer season. He was the star goalie for our high school team. They actually went to the state championships that year, played against a team from up north that had these blindingly bright neon green jerseys. You could see them from a mile away... but yeah, I just felt like I had all the answers and could start that agency without a hitch.\n\nDoctor: During that energized stretch, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess maybe a little? I don't know, it's hard to tell when you're just going and going. But you know who did talk a lot? My high school speech teacher, Mr. Gable. That guy could talk for hours about absolutely nothing. He had this weird habit of jingling the change in his pocket while he lectured, and it drove everyone crazy. It was always pennies and quarters, just clinking away. I tried saving quarters once in this big plastic Coke bottle bank I won at the county fair. The fair was great back then, they had this ride called the Zipper that just flipped you upside down over and over. I threw up a funnel cake on that ride when I was twelve. Powdered sugar everywhere. My mom was so mad she made me ride home in the bed of my dad's pickup truck. It was an old Chevy C10, light blue, with a cracked windshield on the passenger side from a stray golf ball... but yeah, I'm not really sure if I was talking more than usual or not.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you have any hallucinations or beliefs that other people said were not true?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't see anything that wasn't there, or think things that people said were crazy. I mean, my sister always tells me my ideas are crazy, but she's just like that. She's been super critical ever since we were kids. Like this one time, we were building a treehouse in the big oak out back, and she kept saying my knots weren't tied right. I learned how to tie knots in the Boy Scouts, actually. We had this scoutmaster, Mr. Henderson, who used to take us camping up near the lake. The mosquitoes there were just huge, like the size of small birds. I got bit so many times on my ankles that summer I had to wear these thick wool socks even though it was ninety degrees out. Wool socks are the worst when it's hot, they make your feet sweat so much and then they smell like wet dog. We had a dog, a golden retriever named Buster, who loved swimming in that lake... but no, I didn't have anything like what you're asking about.\n\nDoctor: Before your uncle died, had you ever had another period in your life when you felt this unusually energized and needed much less sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, not that I can remember before he passed. His funeral was really hard, actually. We had it at this little church off Route 9, the one with the white steeple that needs painting. The paint was peeling off in these big strips, looked like sunburned skin. I always peel my sunburns, even though you're not supposed to. I got a terrible burn in Florida back in '98. We drove all the way down there in a Honda Civic, four of us crammed in. The AC broke somewhere in Georgia, and we had to roll the windows down, which just blew all this hot, humid air in our faces. My buddy Dave was in the backseat, and he kept complaining about his knees being cramped. He's like six-foot-three, played basketball for a community college in Ohio. They actually made it to some regional tournament, but he sprained his ankle right before the first game. He was so bummed out, he just sat on the bench eating those little bags of pretzels they sell at the concession stand... but yeah, before my uncle, I don't remember ever feeling that revved up or skipping sleep like that.\n\nDoctor: Did this burst of energy and confidence feel clearly unlike the kind of reaction you would normally have after a loss?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I mean, everybody handles grief differently, right? When my aunt passed, my cousin cried for like three weeks straight. She couldn't even go to work. She worked at this bakery downtown, the one that makes those amazing blueberry scones. I used to go there every Tuesday morning before my shift. I'm a personal care aide, so I have to be up pretty early to help my clients get out of bed and dressed. One of my guys, Mr. Higgins, he's got this old basset hound that just howls every time the toaster pops. It's the funniest thing. The dog is practically deaf, but he knows the exact sound of that toaster. He loves toast with a little bit of butter on it. Real butter, not the margarine stuff. Margarine is basically just oil and water anyway, I saw a documentary about how they make it in these massive vats... but yeah, I couldn't really tell you if the way I reacted was normal or not.\n\nDoctor: During those two weeks, did your wife or coworkers say you seemed noticeably different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember my wife or the guys at work saying anything about me acting different. My wife was pretty distracted anyway because we were dealing with this leak in the downstairs bathroom. The pipe under the sink kept dripping, just this constant tap-tap-tap all day. It really gets under your skin. We ended up having to call a plumber, this guy named Mike who drives a bright neon green van. You literally can't miss it. He told me he painted it green because his daughter's favorite color is emerald. She's in a high school marching band, plays the trombone, I think. I tried to play the trumpet back in middle school, but I could never get my lips to do the right thing on the mouthpiece. It just sounded like a dying elephant every time I blew into it. Our band teacher, Mr. Davies, had the worst comb-over I've ever seen in my life, he used to sweat so much during the concerts... but no, I don't think anyone told me I was acting out of the ordinary.\n\nDoctor: During that energized period, did you do anything unusually risky or impulsive?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I did anything really risky. I mean, I'm usually a pretty careful guy. Like, I always check the tire pressure on my car before a long trip. You know, you can get better gas mileage if your tires are inflated right. I read this article once that said most people are driving around on under-inflated tires and wasting all this money on gas. Gas prices are just crazy right now, anyway. I remember when I first started driving, I could fill up my tank for like fifteen bucks. It was a '89 Ford Escort, manual transmission. I loved that car, but the clutch went out on me when I was stuck in traffic on the George Washington Bridge. That was a nightmare. Cars were honking, people were yelling, and I'm just sitting there sweating buckets trying to get it into gear. The tow truck took forever to get there because of the traffic, and the driver was this huge guy who looked like a professional wrestler... but yeah, I don't remember doing anything wild or impulsive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess before I crashed and started feeling this low, there was a stretch where I felt... I don't know, almost too good? Like really powerful, you know? But then the next minute I'd get real sharp with my wife over nothing, or snap at the guys at work. My wife actually said I was acting like a completely different person when we were trying to pick out a new rug for the living room. We went to this huge warehouse store, the kind where you have to have a membership card just to get in the door. I always lose those cards. I must have had them print me like five replacements in the last year. The lady at the customer service desk was giving me this look, you know, like I was the biggest idiot she'd ever seen. She had these bright pink acrylic nails with little rhinestones on them. I don't know how people type with nails like that, it seems like they'd get caught in the keyboard... but anyway, yeah, I'd feel on top of the world and then just snap at her out of nowhere.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.23, F43.9, F31.81\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's nice to meet you too. Um, what brings me in... I guess my head just feels really foggy lately. Like, I'm making these little mistakes in my client notes at work, and I'm a personal care aide, so you know, the notes are important. I was actually just at this one client's house, Mr. Henderson, he's such a sweet older guy, always wants to talk about his time in the Navy. He served on this huge aircraft carrier, I can't remember the name of it now, but he showed me pictures. Anyway, I keep forgetting what I was about to do when I'm there, which really isn't like me at all. I usually have my routine down pat. But yeah, the Navy pictures were really something, black and white, you know? But this foggy feeling, it's just throwing me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, it's just like I was saying with that foggy feeling. I sit down to do my client notes and I just can't focus, so I end up making these little mistakes. Or I'll walk into a room and completely forget what I was about to do. Which really isn't like me at all, you know? I usually have a mind like a steel trap. \n\nMy buddy Dave always says I should've been an accountant because I'm usually so good with details.
